=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array of integers start and an integer d, representing n intervals [start[i], start[i] + d].
You are asked to choose n integers where the i^th integer must belong to the i^th interval. The score of the chosen integers is defined as the minimum absolute difference between any two integers that have been chosen.
Return the maximum possible score of the chosen integers.
 
Example 1:

Input: start = [6,0,3], d = 2
Output: 4
Explanation:
The maximum possible score can be obtained by choosing integers: 8, 0, and 4. The score of these chosen integers is min(|8 - 0|, |8 - 4|, |0 - 4|) which equals 4.

Example 2:

Input: start = [2,6,13,13], d = 5
Output: 5
Explanation:
The maximum possible score can be obtained by choosing integers: 2, 7, 13, and 18. The score of these chosen integers is min(|2 - 7|, |2 - 13|, |2 - 18|, |7 - 13|, |7 - 18|, |13 - 18|) which equals 5.

 
Constraints:

2 <= start.length <= 10^5
0 <= start[i] <= 10^9
0 <= d <= 10^9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxPossibleScore(self, start: List[int], d: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Detailed proof:
Let intervals I_i=[a_i,a_i+d], a_1≤...≤a_n. Suppose selected p_i∈I_i and |p_i-p_j|≥s (if s>0; for s=0 trivial). Let q_1≤...≤q_n be sorted p's. We show q_i∈I_i.

Upper bound: Suppose q_i > a_i+d. Since for all j≤i, a_j≤a_i, so any point in I_j ≤ a_j+d ≤ a_i+d < q_i. Thus the selected points from intervals 1,...,i are all < q_i. That's i points strictly less than q_i. But q_i is the i-th smallest; at most i-1 points can be strictly less than q_i. Contradiction. Wait if duplicates equal q_i? We assumed q_i > a_i+d, and intervals 1..i points < q_i, so at least i points < q_i. At most i-1 points < q_i. Contradiction. Good.

Lower bound: Suppose q_i < a_i. For all j≥i, a_j≥a_i > q_i, so any point in I_j ≥ a_j > q_i. Thus selected points from intervals i,...,n are all > q_i. That's n-i+1 points strictly greater than q_i. But q_i is i-th smallest; at most n-i points can be strictly greater than q_i. Contradiction. Good. Thus q_i∈I_i. Nice. This doesn't even require same d for lower? Upper uses a_j+d ≤ a_i+d for j≤i, requires same d. Lower uses starts sorted. If d same, yes. If d varied, not necessarily. But problem same d. Great.

Now, does existence of sorted q_i with q_i∈I_i and gaps ≥s imply greedy feasible? Yes, q_i is a feasible sorted assignment. Greedy finds minimal possible sequence; if any sequence exists, greedy won't fail. Standard: For constraints x_i ∈ [a_i,b_i], x_i - x_{i-1} ≥ s, sorted by a_i, with b_i=a_i+d. Greedy sets x_i = max(a_i, x_{i-1}+s). If this exceeds b_i, no feasible sequence because any feasible y_i must satisfy y_i ≥ max(a_i, y_{i-1}+s) ≥ max(a_i, x_{i-1}+s)? Need induction: x_i ≤ y_i for all i if y feasible. Base x_0=a_0≤y_0. If x_{i-1}≤y_{i-1}, then x_i=max(a_i,x_{i-1}+s) ≤ max(a_i,y_{i-1}+s) ≤ y_i (since y_i≥a_i and y_i≥y_{i-1}+s). Thus if greedy x_i > b_i, y_i ≥ x_i > b_i impossible. So greedy correct. Good.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxPossibleScore(self, start: List[int], d: int) -> int:
        start.sort()
        n = len(start)
        def ok(s: int) -> bool:
            prev = start[0]
            for i in range(1, n):
                cur = start[i]
                if prev + s > cur:
                    cur = prev + s
                # equivalently cur = max(cur, prev+s)
                if cur > start[i] + d:
                    return False
                prev = cur
            return True

        lo = 0
        hi = (start[-1] + d - start[0]) + 1
        while lo + 1 < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if ok(mid):
                lo = mid
            else:
                hi = mid
        return lo

Need check `prev + s` can be large but Python ok. If s=0, cur = max(start[i], prev) = start[i] because sorted, ok. If start[i]+d maybe int. Good.

Could optimize by using `upper = start[i] + d` each loop; no issue.

Let's maybe include in final code only, no explanation. But the user asked "Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way" and "Output only final Python code enclosed in a code block." So final should be code block only. We can include code.

Need ensure code uses `List` imported. The final code:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxPossibleScore(self, start: List[int], d: int) -> int:
        start.sort()
        n = len(start)

        def can(score: int) -> bool:
            prev = start[0]
            for i in range(1, n):
                cur = max(start[i], prev + score)
                if cur > start[i] + d:
                    return False
                prev = cur
            return True

        lo, hi = 0, start[-1] + d - start[0] + 1
        while lo + 1 < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if can(mid):
                lo = mid
            else:
                hi = mid
        return lo
```

This avoids sorting in place and makes clear we use sorted. It uses O(n) memory. The problem constraints 1e5, memory fine. Maybe better practice. Let's decide. The starter code has `start` parameter; sorting in place is common. But if we sort in place, the original intervals are lost; not needed. I think either is fine. To avoid side effects and potential confusion, use `st = sorted(start)`. But then we have an extra list; no issue. The final code can be:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxPossibleScore(self, start: List[int], d: int) -> int:
        st = sorted(start)
        n = len(st)

        def can(score: int) -> bool:
            prev = st[0]
            for i in range(1, n):
                cur = max(st[i], prev + score)
                if cur > st[i] + d:
                    return False
                prev = cur
            return True

        lo, hi = 0, st[-1] + d - st[0] + 1
        while lo + 1 < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if can(mid):
                lo = mid
            else:
                hi = mid
        return lo
```

This is slightly more memory but okay. Which one to output? Either. Using `st = sorted(start)` avoids modifying input and maybe more readable. Let's use `st` to be safe. The problem constraints memory 1e5 ints ~2.8MB, fine. Sorting in place also memory less but not important.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "can" if `score` = 33 and st=[0,0,0,0,1000], d=100: prev0; i1 33
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
.

The code sorts `start`, then binary-searches the maximum possible score. For a candidate `score`, it greedily places each chosen integer as early as possible while keeping at least `score` distance from the previous one. If any placement exceeds its interval, the candidate is impossible.

This is correct for the problem.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxPossibleScore(self, start: List[int], d: int) -> int:
        st = sorted(start)
        n = len(st)

        def can(score: int) -> bool:
            prev = st[0]
            for i in range(1, n):
                cur = max(st[i], prev + score)
                if cur > st[i] + d:
                    return False
                prev = cur
            return True

        lo, hi = 0, st[-1] + d - st[0] + 1
        while lo + 1 < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if can(mid):
                lo = mid
            else:
                hi = mid

        return lo
```
